# supabase/tests/card_payment_benchmark/removal/run.sh   (2026-10-09, I3A-R packet 1 — removal provenance. "current" is the 20261008120000 body cc12b7a12c6510924781b93c658ee854 with its two tables and their guard triggers; it is measured against "revision5", the 20261006120000 body 7997a757408fa888aa44bdbc9d85f89d, in the same runs and the same rounds; "previous" is the 20260926120000 body a8f567d397b9dae5b777497317985ab7; evaluator 6cb5e299b0383fab0b6517cc2e7522c8; local, synthetic; six fixture variants)
#
# WHAT THIS IS. Local measurements on synthetic data in throwaway containers. NOT hosted latency, not a pass/fail test, never
# correctness evidence. The counts are checked against what each fixture variant describes (the script stops otherwise); the
# timings are what this machine did on this night.
#
# THE BYTES. supabase/migrations/20261008120000_card_payment_removal_provenance.sql md5 f840ef9e6bbb97cfb94df1c756a4760f;
# bench.cjs md5 bafc8f9d967bdf4333799f84fe07ac00, run.sh md5 2d92445899f0382adde0f1690a8b0f41 — the same at the start and at
# the end of every run below (each raw log records them). (run.sh has since gained one word in a header comment, the
# name BENCH_BACKGROUND; no statement of it changed.) The body checksums are not written in the harness: bench.cjs takes
# the function text from the migration files, checks it against the checksum each file's own postcondition names, and the
# generated script refuses to run unless the installed function is that body.
#
# THE MACHINE, AND HOW FAR THE TIMINGS CAN BE TRUSTED.
#   * Docker here reports 8 CPUs. Between one and three OTHER test containers (access-control, rehearsal and evaluator
#     harnesses of the same validation) were busy during every run; each run lists them at its start and its end. The
#     timings are therefore SLOW and NOISY. A measure of how slow: an interrupted pass earlier the same night, on a quieter
#     machine, timed the unchanged revision5 body on the dense fixture at 377 / 630 / 1446 ms (sizes 2 / 4 / 8); here the same
#     code on the same fixture takes 966 / 1498 / 3199 ms. The first three runs (dense, cycles, same_date: three other
#     containers) are 2.2 to 2.6 times slower than that pass; the later ones (one other container) about 1.5 times (base,
#     revision5: 206 / 1,361 ms here, 137 / 964 ms then). Single samples of one phase differ by up to 2x.
#   * So read, in this order: (1) the COUNTS and the WORK counters (buffers, WAL: pg_stat_statements), which do not depend on
#     the load; (2) the comparison ROUND BY ROUND — the two bodies of one round ran one directly after the other, on fixtures
#     built the same way — and its ratio; (3) medians; (4) p95, which with 5 samples is an interpolation between the two
#     slowest and says little more than "the slowest sample".
#   * 5 samples after 1 warm-up for every fixture; 3 after 1 where the run says so. Every sample is printed.
#
# WHAT CHANGED IN bench.cjs FOR THIS FILE.
#   * It used to slice statements (a), (b), (c) out of the 20261006120000 file while timing whatever function was
#     installed. Its "current" is now bound to 20261008120000: every statement of that body's preservation block is sliced
#     from that file's function text — the markers, the generation, the plan table's DDL (created and emptied INSIDE the
#     timed transaction, as the function does), the three plan inserts, the three decision inserts, the provenance insert —
#     and the generator stops if the block contains anything it does not time. The 20261006120000 function is installed
#     beside it as "revision5" and measured as a body of its own, so that the ADDED cost is a measured difference.
#   * The lookups of the two guard triggers are sliced from the same file (the generator stops if a guard gains one). The
#     three lookups of the source guard are run AGAIN for every provenance row, each in a loop of its own, between the
#     provenance insert and the delete: what each costs per row. Their plans are printed.
#   * A removal is now timed as one committed transaction that takes the user's advisory lock first; "lock held to commit"
#     is the same sample from the lock's grant to COMMIT's return. An evaluation and a read are timed after every removal.
#   * Four new fixture variants (provenance_heavy, same_date, dense, cycles) beside base and hold_heavy, which are unchanged.
#   * BENCH_BACKGROUND=<users>: other users in the database, so that no table the new statements read is a few rows long.
#   * Bytes are reported in BOTH measures: the sum of pg_column_size over the rows written, and the growth of
#     pg_total_relation_size (heap and indexes) of each of the three tables after a VACUUM FULL.
#
# THE RUNS (2026-10-09 UTC; one container at a time; in this order). Sizes that were REDUCED are marked, with the full command.
#   dense             02:33-02:43  BENCH_FIXTURE=dense run.sh 5 1 2,4,8                              3 other containers busy
#   cycles            02:43-03:03  BENCH_FIXTURE=cycles BENCH_ROWS=500 run.sh 5 1 0,1,3,5            3 other containers busy
#                     REDUCED: 500 ordinary rows on each institution instead of the default 2000 (the fixture of one sample
#                     runs up to five removals before the measured one). Full: BENCH_FIXTURE=cycles run.sh 5 1 0,1,3,5
#   same_date         03:03-03:18  BENCH_FIXTURE=same_date run.sh 5 1 2,8,32                         3, then 1
#   provenance_heavy  03:18-03:22  BENCH_FIXTURE=provenance_heavy run.sh 5 1 1000,10000              1
#   hold_heavy        03:22-03:26  BENCH_FIXTURE=hold_heavy run.sh 5 1 1000,10000                    1
#   base              03:26-03:30  BENCH_FIXTURE=base run.sh 5 1 1000,10000                          1
#   provenance_heavy  03:30-03:43  BENCH_FIXTURE=provenance_heavy run.sh 3 1 50000                   1
#   same_date + 2000  03:43-04:02  BENCH_FIXTURE=same_date BENCH_BACKGROUND=2000 run.sh 3 1 2,8,32   1
#                     the plans and the guard with 2,000 other users in the database; 3 samples
#   base              04:02-04:12  BENCH_FIXTURE=base run.sh 3 1 50000                               1
#   hold_heavy        04:12-04:25  BENCH_FIXTURE=hold_heavy run.sh 3 1 50000                         1
#   hold_heavy again  04:27-04:38  the same command, once more: its first run was disturbed           0, then 1
#                     REDUCED, these three variants: size 50000 was run on its own with 3 samples instead of 5, to bound the
#                     time on a shared machine. Full, all three sizes with 5 samples (the defaults), for each variant:
#                     BENCH_FIXTURE=base run.sh    BENCH_FIXTURE=hold_heavy run.sh    BENCH_FIXTURE=provenance_heavy run.sh
# (run.sh = supabase/tests/card_payment_benchmark/removal/run.sh; its arguments are samples, warm-ups, sizes.)
#
# ============================================================================================================================
# WHAT THE MEASUREMENTS SAY
# ============================================================================================================================
#
# 1. WHERE NOTHING IS MERGED, THE NEW BODY WRITES THE SAME DECISIONS AND COSTS A FEW PERCENT MORE.
#    base, hold_heavy, provenance_heavy and the first removal of cycles: one removed candidate per surviving payment, no
#    earlier removal. revision5 and current write the same decisions (10 to 2,500 at these sizes; table A); current adds one
#    generation row and one provenance row per decision.
#      * The whole removal, round by round (table B): between -11 ms and +50 ms, ratio 0.96-1.03, at 1000 and 10000 of base,
#        hold_heavy and provenance_heavy: inside the spread of the samples. At 50000, with 3 samples each: base -70 ms
#        (x0.99); provenance_heavy +0.99 s (x1.08; the three rounds read -0.47, +0.99 and +2.64 s); hold_heavy +3.28 s (x1.41;
#        -0.88, +3.28, +6.59 s) and, run a second time, -0.09 s (x0.99; -1.52, -0.09, +2.69 s). A removal of that size takes
#        7 to 17 s here and its samples are scattered by seconds: in the hold_heavy run that reads x1.41, the evaluation
#        AFTER the removal — identical work under both bodies, 615,642 against 615,865 buffers — reads x1.60 in the same
#        rounds. (The first removal of cycles, measured while three other containers were busy, reads +185 ms, x1.20, three
#        rounds of five slower.) Whole-removal timing cannot resolve what is added at these shapes. The statements one by
#        one show it, and the work counters show it without the noise:
#      * the preservation block takes 32.5 against 21.7 ms (base 1000, 10 decisions), 64.7 against 37.2 (base 10000, 100),
#        104.9 against 62.7 (hold_heavy 10000, 200), 128.4 against 70.3 (provenance_heavy 10000, 500), 209 against 135 (base
#        50000, 500), 379 and 463 against 248 (hold_heavy 50000, 1,000: its two runs), 743 against 497 (provenance_heavy
#        50000, 2,500): about 10 ms that do not depend on the size — the generation with its guard (2-4 ms), the plan
#        table's CREATE and TRUNCATE (3-5 ms here), three more statements to plan — and 0.1 to 0.2 ms per decision (its
#        provenance row, and the detour through the plan table);
#      * WAL of the whole call: +5 % (base), +8 % (hold_heavy), +10 to +12 % (provenance_heavy), at every size; +630 to
#        +880 bytes per decision. Shared buffers: +4 to +14 %.
#      * After it, an evaluation and a read cost the same: they go through the same live rows and do the same work (table
#        E). Their ratios are noise: 0.87-1.15 at 1000 and 10000, with one of 1.47 on a read of 7 ms; 0.94-1.02 at 50000
#        apart from the disturbed hold_heavy run.
#    Against the body before 20261006120000 nothing changes in the picture recorded on 2026-10-07: the evaluation and the
#    delete are the removal's cost (base 10000: previous 494 ms, revision5 1,361 ms, current 1,130 ms in this run; base 50000:
#    3.13, 6.98 and 7.12 s).
#
# 2. WHERE REVISION 5 MERGED, THE NEW BODY WRITES MORE ROWS, AND THE REMOVAL IS AS MUCH LONGER AS THERE ARE ROWS.
#    That is the contract (one hold per owner, removed candidate and kept candidate), and row growth is its price.
#      holds written by one removal        revision5                     current
#      same_date, R = 2 / 8 / 32           400 / 400 / 400               800 / 3,200 / 12,800      (100 x 4 x R: linear in R)
#      dense, n = 2 / 4 / 8                80 / 320 / 1,280 (20 n^2)     160 / 1,280 / 10,240      (20 n^3)
#    In general one removal writes, for every surviving leg o that has no automatic match with a leg that stays,
#    (removed candidates of o) x (kept candidates of o) holds; revision5 wrote (distinct DATES among the removed candidates
#    of o) x (kept candidates of o), less those already live. A candidate is a leg of exactly the opposite amount within
#    five days, so both factors are bounded only by how many such legs the user has. Where owners, removed candidates and
#    kept candidates of one amount all grow together (dense), the rows grow with the CUBE of that number; revision5's grew
#    with the square. That is the only growth here that is faster than linear, and it is in the number of ROWS: per row,
#    time and work are flat —
#      * preservation block per hold written: 414 / 363 / 357 microseconds (same_date 2 / 8 / 32);
#        WAL per additional hold: 1,663 and 1,688 bytes (same_date 2->8, 8->32); 1,757 and 1,533 (dense 2->4, 4->8);
#        shared buffers per additional hold: 72 and 74 (same_date).
#    The removal, median and round by round:
#      same_date 2 / 8 / 32    2.05 / 3.93 / 17.5 s   against  1.92 / 2.67 / 7.41 s    x1.16 / x1.57 / x2.20
#      dense 2 / 4 / 8         1.14 / 3.16 / 13.8 s   against  0.97 / 1.50 / 3.20 s    x1.23 / x1.67 / x4.14
#    (a busy machine: about 2.5 times the times of a quiet one, see above. The ratios move with the load too: the same
#    dense command with 3 samples and one other container busy read x1.16 / x1.69 / x3.61 for the removal — the packet's
#    evidence, logs/verify-perf/bench_dense_3x1_verify.log, the same bytes. Read them as ranges: about x1.2, x1.7 and
#    x3.6 to x4.1 for dense; direction and order of magnitude hold, a single ratio moves by 15 to 50 %. The counts and the
#    work counters reproduced within 0.3 %.)
#    The added time is in three places (table C, same_date 32): the decision inserts, 1.95 s for 12,800 rows where revision5's
#    one statement took 0.25 s for 400; the provenance insert, 2.35 s; and the FINAL evaluation, 3.49 s against 0.61 s,
#    because it now reads 12,800 live holds. The first evaluation is the same under both bodies and is the largest single
#    cost at this shape (5.5-6.0 s at R = 32): that is the evaluator on clusters of same-amount legs, not this packet.
#    These two fixtures are stress shapes: 100 amounts with up to 32 payments of exactly one amount on one day on the removed
#    card, or 20 amounts with 8 + 8 + 8 legs inside five days. What they show is the slope, not a typical user.
#
# 3. THE LOCK. "lock held to commit" is the removal's own duration less about one millisecond, in every row of table B: the
#    function takes the user's advisory lock first and everything it does is under it; from its first evaluation on it also
#    holds the user's version row. So every millisecond this packet adds is added to the time every other writer of that user
#    waits: nothing measurable at the shapes of section 1, and x2.2 / x4.1 at the largest stress shapes — 17.5 s and 13.8 s here.
#    STATEMENT AND LOCK TIMEOUTS. No sample here could have been cancelled: the benchmark session connects as
#    supabase_admin and SETs ROLE service_role, SET ROLE applies no role's settings, and "show statement_timeout" in that
#    session is 0. The image the harness runs in does configure timeouts for the roles an API request starts as:
#    authenticator statement_timeout=8s and lock_timeout=8s; authenticated 8s; anon 3s; service_role none; nothing at
#    database level (read in a benchmark container of these bytes: logs/verify-perf/role_timeouts_in_the_image.txt in the
#    packet's evidence). The backend calls the function through the API (backend/src/services/dataService.ts,
#    supabaseAdmin.rpc). NOT established here, and the repository owner's to confirm: whether the API layer applies the
#    authenticator's 8 s to a request made as service_role, and what the hosted project is configured with.
#    Table B has to be read against 8 s all the same, for BOTH bodies. On this busy machine revision5 already runs
#    7.0 / 8.5 / 15.8 s at 50000 (base / hold_heavy / provenance_heavy) and 7.4 s at same_date 32; current runs
#    7.1 / 10.0 to 11.3 / 16.8 s, and 17.5 s and 13.8 s at same_date 32 and dense 8. If a statement timeout of 8 s applies,
#    a removal that deterministically runs longer can never complete (a retry does the same work), and while a removal
#    holds the user's advisory lock for more than 8 s, another writer of that user waiting under lock_timeout=8s is
#    cancelled. That exposure exists under revision5 at the largest ordinary shapes; at the two stress shapes this packet
#    adds to it (x2.2 and about x3.6 to x4.1). A quiet machine is about 2.5 times faster than these figures.
#
# 4. EARLIER REMOVALS: ROWS ACCUMULATE, AND EVERYTHING THAT GOES THROUGH THEM GROWS WITH THEM. NOTHING BOUNDS IT.
#    cycles: one institution, removed, linked again (new ids, the same legs) and removed again, P times, by the body under
#    test; then the measured removal. 100 payments to the removed card, 2 kept competitors each: 300 decisions a removal.
#      * current writes its 300 decisions and 300 provenance rows on EVERY removal; revision5 wrote them once and nothing
#        afterwards (table G). Live system rows after the measured removal: 300 / 600 / 1,200 / 1,800 for P = 0 / 1 / 3 / 5;
#        under revision5, 300 throughout.
#      * The work grows by the same amount for every earlier removal, i.e. linearly (table F and the raw counts):
#          the removal itself       shared buffers 47,138 / 53,132 / 65,666 / 77,656 (+6,100 each); WAL 1.48 / 1.67 / 2.06 /
#                                   2.45 MB (+193 kB each) — its two evaluations go through every live system row;
#          one evaluation after     51,301 / 63,795 / 88,596 / 113,696 buffers (+12,500 each: about 42 per live system row;
#                                   same_date and dense: 41 to 47 per additional live hold);
#          one read after           the answer lists every live decision: 323 / 362 / 440 / 518 kB (+39 kB each: 130 bytes
#                                   per row).
#      * Timings, this (busy) run: removal 1.13 / 1.76 / 2.19 / 2.56 s against 0.98 / 1.06 / 1.46 / 1.27 s, round by round
#        x1.20 / x1.53 / x1.43 / x2.02; evaluation after x1.24 / x1.68 / x1.39 / x1.98; read after x0.86 / x1.44 / x1.03 /
#        x1.30. The samples of this run are too scattered to read a slope from them (revision5, which does the same work at
#        every P, reads 0.98 to 1.46 s). A quieter pass ON THESE BYTES (the same reduced command with 3 samples, one other
#        container busy: the packet's evidence, logs/verify-perf/bench_cycles_rows500_3x1_verify.log) read
#        819 / 891 / 1,006 / 1,295 ms against 715 / 729 / 748 / 739 ms: about +95 ms of removal for every earlier removal
#        whose 300 rows are still live; the evaluation after it 417 / 394 / 514 / 629 against 340 / 347 / 380 / 349 ms; the
#        read after it 32.5 / 37.5 / 52.9 / 68.0 against 35.1 / 37.7 / 34.4 / 33.7 ms.
#      * Earlier removals that ran WITHOUT provenance ("current, prior unrecorded": revision5 ran them): the measured removal
#        writes one marker for each (0 / 1 / 3 / 5; 80 bytes each). The marker statement took 1.8 ms for five of them, their
#        guard and their foreign-key checks included (its plan, below). Its work is that of a second removal (53,988
#        buffers at P = 5 against 52,789 at P = 1), since revision5 left one set of rows however often it ran.
#    UNBOUNDED, plainly: (a) the number of live system rows of a user grows by one full set with every relink-and-remove
#    cycle and nothing in this packet retires a row — they go only with the surviving account or the user; (b) with them
#    grow, linearly and without a bound, both evaluations inside every later removal of that user, every later evaluation,
#    and every read and the size of its answer; (c) the rows one removal writes have no cap either (section 2). Bounded:
#    the generations (one row per removal, one marker per earlier unrecorded removal, once) and the plan table (dropped
#    at commit).
#
# 5. BYTES (both measures; table A).
#      * a provenance row: 203 bytes (pg_column_size; 211 in cycles, whose Plaid ids are longer); on disk, heap and its
#        three indexes, 280 to 330 bytes a row once the table has more than a few pages (2,867,200 / 10,240 dense 8;
#        4,186,112 / 12,800 same_date 32);
#      * the decision row it explains: 168 bytes (a record) or 244 (a hold); on disk 325 bytes a hold with its four indexes;
#      * so a system row costs 1.9 to 2.0 times the disk it cost under revision5, and where revision5 merged there are R (or
#        n) times as many of them: same_date 32 adds 8.4 MB to the two tables where revision5 added 0.19 MB; dense 8, 6.2 MB
#        against 0.43 MB;
#      * a generation: 77-80 bytes; the first one takes five pages (the heap and four indexes, 40,960 bytes), later ones
#        nothing measurable.
#    WAL is the larger number while a removal runs: 22.8 MB against 4.5 MB (same_date 32), 16.5 against 2.9 MB (dense 8).
#
# 6. THE GUARD OF card_payment_removal_sources, AND ITS NEW LOOKUP (table D).
#    The guard runs once for every provenance row, inside the provenance insert, under both locks. It makes three lookups;
#    the third — the removed transaction, by its Plaid id, on the named account of the item being removed — was added last.
#    Replayed once per provenance row right after the insert (the trigger's own statements, one statement per row):
#      * lookup 1 (the decision): 8-36 microseconds a row; lookup 2 (the generation of this transaction, the removal, the
#        account): 17-45; lookup 3 (the removed transaction): 28-61, in every run of 100 rows or more — 37 at same_date 32,
#        51 at dense 8, 29 and 28 at provenance_heavy 10000 and 50000;
#      * the provenance statement as a whole costs 116-451 microseconds a row at those sizes, so the new lookup is 10-26 %
#        of the statement (20 % at same_date 32) and the three lookups together a quarter to a half of it. EXPLAIN
#        (ANALYZE) of the statement, one sample with its instrumentation, puts the whole guard — the lookups and
#        PL/pgSQL's own call — at 48-63 % of the statement (1,360 of 2,830 ms at dense 8; 3,086 of 5,304 ms at same_date
#        32; 74 of 118 ms at provenance_heavy 10000); the three foreign-key checks of the row are another 20-37 %;
#      * in a whole removal the new lookup is small: 0.48 s of 17.5 s at same_date 32 (12,800 rows), 0.52 s of 13.8 s at
#        dense 8, 14 ms of 2.1 s at provenance_heavy 10000. It grows with the provenance rows and with nothing else.
#    Its plan: transactions by transactions_plaid_transaction_id_key (unique), then the account and the removal's ledger row
#    by their keys; one row read from each. In this one-user database the account and the ledger row are read by
#    sequential scans of tables that are four and one to six rows long; with other users in the database (the background
#    run) every lookup of both guards goes by an index and reads the one row it needs. No lookup scans a table that grows
#    with the database.
#    WITH 2,000 OTHER USERS IN THE DATABASE (same_date again, BENCH_BACKGROUND=2000: 4,000 accounts, 4,000 transactions, 2,000
#    ledger rows and generations, 4,000 decisions and 4,000 provenance rows of other users before the fixture; 3 samples):
#      * every lookup of both guards goes by an index (plans below), and costs what it cost by scans of one-row tables:
#        16 / 31 / 41 microseconds a row at R = 32 against 15 / 29 / 37; the statement 194 against 184; the new lookup 21 %
#        of it (26 % and 21 % at R = 2 and 8);
#      * the removal's ratio to revision5 is what it was: x1.09 / x1.56 / x1.94 (2.24 / 4.65 / 15.7 s against 2.20 / 2.98 /
#        8.10 s), and so are the rows, the bytes and the WAL;
#      * what does change with other users is in section 7: a statement both bodies share.
#
# 7. THE PLANS OF THE NEW STATEMENTS (printed for the largest size of every run; kept in the raw logs, the important ones
#    below). No missing index was found. With other users in the database two things show that a one-user database cannot:
#    a statement both bodies share gets a join order that costs seven times as much, and the provenance insert reads two
#    permanent tables whole while they are small (both below, after the statements).
#      * markers: the user's removals by plaid_item_removals_user_id_idx, anti-joined to card_payment_removal_generations by
#        its (removal_id, user_id) key, index-only. In the one-user runs both tables are a few rows and are scanned.
#      * generation: one row. Its guard reads the ledger row by key or through the user's few ledger rows
#        (plaid_item_removals_pkey, plaid_item_removals_user_id_idx: the planner used either) and the user's last ordinal by an
#        index-only backward scan of (user_id, ordinal): one index entry, however many removals the user has.
#      * plan table: CREATE TEMPORARY TABLE IF NOT EXISTS + TRUNCATE, 3 to 9 ms a removal on this machine: catalog work.
#      * plan (a), (b), (c): the SELECTs of 20261006120000 with their joins and predicates, into the plan table. (c) no
#        longer probes card_payment_decisions for a live hold with the same pair and date. Their cost is the evaluator's
#        scratch tables: 257 ms for 12,800 planned holds, 20 microseconds a row — in the one-user database.
#      * the three decision inserts: three sequential scans of the plan table (it has no index but its key, and needs
#        none: it holds this removal's rows only) and a sort each. Per row the cost is card_payment_decisions' own
#        triggers, as before: at 12,800 holds (EXPLAIN ANALYZE) three foreign-key checks 20-29 microseconds each, the version
#        bump 64, the same-user check 93.
#      * provenance insert: the plan table, joined to accounts and to transactions by transactions_pkey; the lateral
#        count over transactions is never executed for a system row (its one-time filter); transaction_carryovers is
#        EMPTY in every fixture of this file, so its join was only ever planned against an empty table here (a scan of
#        it). With the table populated — a scratch run on these bytes, 500 other users and 5,000 unconsumed carry-overs,
#        same_date 32: logs/verify-perf/plans_same_date32_bg500_with_carryovers.log — the planner uses a Hash Left Join
#        on (account_id, pending_plaid_transaction_id) after ONE sequential scan of all unconsumed carry-overs (7 ms for
#        5,000): no nested loop, but a scan that grows with the database, like the two described below.
#        Then, per row, three foreign-key checks and the guard.
#    SEEN IN THE PLANS ONCE THE DATABASE HAS OTHER USERS — and not in any earlier results file, whose database had one:
#      * Statement (c)'s SELECT — 20261006120000's, which this packet keeps as it is — gets another join order, UNDER BOTH
#        BODIES. In the one-user runs it restricts the removed and the kept candidates to their institutions first (the
#        user's four accounts and two items are the whole of those tables) and produces its 12,800 rows directly. With
#        2,000 other users it first joins every owner to ALL its candidates, twice — 130,000 combinations at R = 32,
#        (R + 4)^2 for each owner — and only then looks up each candidate's account and item by key: 130,000 and 115,200
#        probes of accounts_pkey and twice 115,200 of plaid_items_pkey, to keep 12,800. No sequential scan; about 1.5 million
#        more buffer hits in the removal (revision5: 1,595,478 against 112,854; current: 2,761,038 against 976,570).
#        The statement takes 1.86 s under revision5 and 1.89 s under current at R = 32, against 0.25 and 0.26 s in the
#        one-user run (R = 8: 239 and 221 ms against 131 and 81; R = 2: 147 and 39 against 88 and 26). Its work then grows
#        with the SQUARE of an owner's candidates even where its output grows linearly (here: R^2 against R). The
#        evaluator's scratch tables have no statistics, so the planner cannot know that the institution is the selective
#        condition. Not added by this packet and equal for both bodies, so it is in none of the differences above; but
#        every one-user timing of statement (c), in this file and in the earlier ones, understates it.
#      * The plan table is never analyzed either. The provenance insert reads it from a default estimate, and with 2,000
#        other users it hashes the WHOLE of public.accounts (4,004 rows) and of public.transactions (11,700 rows) for its
#        12,800 planned rows instead of probing them by key (19 ms of 2.4 s). PostgreSQL prices that scan against the
#        probes it replaces, so it is chosen only while those tables are small next to the plan; in every one-user run,
#        where transactions holds 1,600 to 100,000 rows of one user, it probes transactions_pkey.
#    Also seen, and harmless: card_payment_removal_generations_user_idx (user_id) repeats the leading column of the unique key
#    card_payment_removal_generations_user_ordinal_key (user_id, ordinal). The planner uses it (the guard's lookup 2 reads
#    the user's generations through it) where the unique key would serve as well: one more index entry per removal.
#    And one that predates this packet and belongs to no statement it adds: public.accounts has no index on item_id, so
#    "accounts where item_id = <item>" (the removal's lock on the item's account rows, its counts, the cascade of the
#    delete) scans the table under every body.
#
